 SECTION 1.0
 INTRODUCTION

1.1 Background

 About 50 million gallons of high-level mixed waste is currently stored in underground
tanks at The United States Department of Energy’s (DOE’s) Hanford site in the State of
Washington. The Hanford Tank Waste Treatment and Immobilization Plant (WTP) will provide
DOE’s Office of River Protection (ORP) with a means of treating this waste by vitrification for
subsequent disposal. The tank waste will be separated into low- and high-activity waste
fractions, which will then be vitrified respectively into Immobilized Low Activity Waste
(ILAW) and Immobilized High Level Waste (IHLW) products. The ILAW product will be
disposed in an engineered facility – the Integrated Disposal Facility (IDF) – on the Hanford site,
while the IHLW product is designed for deep geological disposal in a national facility for high-
level nuclear waste. The ILAW and IHLW products must meet a variety of requirements with
respect to protection of the environment before they can be accepted for disposal. The objective
of the work described in this report is to perform testing, data collection, and analyses for the
ILAW glass product for subsequent use in the performance assessment (PA) of the IDF to assess
potential environmental risks associated with long-term storage.

 The technical approach used to predict long-term release from ILAW glass for the IDF
PA has been developed, refined, and updated over the past two decades. The approach and the
supporting test data for ILAW glasses have been extensively documented [1-6]. The approach
has gained acceptance from the Low-Level Waste Disposal Facility Federal Review Group
(LFRG) and the international community evaluating the long-term corrosion of waste glass. It
has also been peer reviewed and approved by a panel of independent experts during development
of the 1998 IDF PA. The underlying premise of the IDF PA is that the source term for
radionuclide release is controlled by the long-term weathering of the glass matrix. This
engineering-based approach has been used in previous PAs to provide the defense-in-depth
required to support the above premise and demonstrate through computer simulations that the
proposed glass waste form will meet the regulatory requirements. The key component of this
engineering-based approach is the estimation of the model parameters and chemical reaction
network needed to provide a robust simulation of glass weathering and the corresponding release
of radionuclides over the period of performance (~10,000 years).

 The present work is part of an ongoing program to conduct the necessary testing to
permit the estimation of the model parameters for representative low activity waste (LAW)
glasses. These parameters are used to populate a kinetic rate law that describes the release from
the glass waste form. The rate law, which is based on chemical affinity and Transition State
Theory (TST) [7], is given by [1-6]:

 8

The Catholic University of America FY2016 ILAW Glass Testing for Disposal at IDF
Vitreous State Laboratory Final Report, VSL-17R3860-1, Rev. 0

                 r  k0 10 . pH T exp( Ea / RT )[1  (Q / K g )]  rIEX                            (1.1)

 where r is the glass dissolution rate (g m-2 d-1), R is the gas constant (kJ/(mo1 K)), T is
the temperature (K), Q is the ion activity product (unitless), and σ is the Temkin coefficient
which has been theoretically shown to equal one. The model parameters k0 ,, Ea , Kg , and rIEX
are the forward rate constant k0 (g m-2 d-1), the pH power law coefficient η, the apparent
activation energy Ea (kJ/mol), and the pseudo-equilibrium constant Kg for the rate controlling
phase (or phases). The parameters can be deduced from experimental measurements by varying
the effects of: (1) pH, (2) temperature, (3) saturation state of the system, and (4) the activities of
species that affect the rate of glass dissolution via Q. Equation 1.1 is based on the kinetic rate
equation developed by Åagaard and Helgeson [8], as applied to glass by Grambow [9] wherein
orthosilicic acid is the only rate controlling species in Q. In addition to the TST rate equation, a
further term has been added to the IDF PA calculations to predict the ion-exchange rate (rIEX) as
the glass-water reaction proceeds. In general, the ion exchange rate is a function of temperature,
glass composition, and reaction progress (stage of the glass-water reaction). The potential
importance of the contribution from ion exchange in the long-term corrosion mechanism had
been emphasized previously by Feng and Pegg [10-12]. This parameter has been estimated by
comparing the boron and sodium dissolution rates; measurement of its temperature dependence
also permits estimation of the activation energy of the Na ion exchange reaction EIEX.

 The use of Equation 1.1 with suitably estimated parameters allows for the source-term to
be estimated within a reactive chemical transport modeling framework that takes into account the
coupled effects of fluid flow and glass-water reactions on the chemistry of fluids percolating
through the disposal facility. Coupling the fluid chemistry with a kinetic rate equation allows the
simulations to describe the response of the glass corrosion rate to changes in fluid composition as
a function of time and space.

 In the early IDF PAs, three or four prototypic glasses that spanned the range of glass
compositions expected to be produced by the WTP were selected based upon specific processing
constraints and composition projections that were current at the time. However, subsequent work
has expanded the range of glass compositions that may be produced at the WTP and this type of
composition range expansion is likely to continue through the life of the project. Thus, while the
basic technical approach is well developed, there is a need to expand the range of glasses tested
in order to span the composition range expected to be disposed of in the IDF and to develop an
understanding of the dependence of the underlying model parameters and reaction network on
the ILAW glass composition. Washington River Protection Solutions, LLC (WRPS) is leading
the work to address this need and contracted with Atkins and the Vitreous State Laboratory
(VSL) of The Catholic University of America (CUA) to provide scientific and technical support.
The present report describes results from testing performed according to a Test Plan [13] that is
responsive to the corresponding WRPS scope of work [14]. The scope of the present work was to
determine the kinetic parameters η, Ea, Kg, and rIEX for a selected high waste loading ILAW glass
(IDF7-E12) through analysis of data obtained from glass dissolution using the single-pass flow-
through (SPFT) test.

 9

The Catholic University of America FY2016 ILAW Glass Testing for Disposal at IDF
Vitreous State Laboratory Final Report, VSL-17R3860-1, Rev. 0

1.2 Test Overview

 In the present work a full suite of SPFT tests was performed on a single high waste
loading LAW glass (IDF7-E12) in order to determine kinetic law parameters for that glass. The
work included:

  Glass dissolution under dilute conditions1 at pH(23°C) values of 7, 8, 9, 10, 11, and 12
 and temperatures of 23, 40, 70, and 90°C.

  Glass dissolution under increasingly saturating conditions2 at pH(23°C) 9 and
           temperatures of 23, 40, 70, and 90°C.

  Data analysis to determine the values of the kinetic parameters k0, η, Ea, Kg, and rIEX of
 Equation 1.1.

1.3 Report Outline

 In order to provide context for the interpretation and potential mechanistic implications of
the collected data and to assess how the data fit within the current knowledge of glass corrosion,
a brief survey of the relevant literature is presented in Section 2. The test matrix for the present
work is described in Section 3. Section 4 describes the selection and preparation of the IDF7-E12
glass. The testing and data analysis are described in Section 5. The results are presented and
discussed in Section 6, while Section 7 provides the summary and conclusions.

1
 Flow rate /surface ratio or q/S ~10-8 m/s
2
 Increasing concentrations of orthosilicic acid in the influent solutions

 SECTION 2.0
 REVIEW OF GLASS DISSOLUTION

2.1 Classical Understanding of Glass Dissolution

 Aqueous dissolution of borosilicate glasses, relevant to nuclear waste disposal, has been
studied, reported, and debated extensively over the past 40 years [15-19]. The general consensus
reached can be summarized as follows:

  For silicate and borosilicate glasses corrosion in the presence of water is the outcome of
 interrelated reactions that take place mainly on the surface of the glass. Glasses consist of
 highly crosslinked networks of Si atoms held together via bridging bonds with oxygen
 atoms (also referred to as bridging oxygens or BOs). Other elements such as Al, Zr, and
 B are also known to participate in the network formation, whereas cations such as Li, Na,
 and K, when present in amounts in excess of those that can still support bridging oxygens
 along with trivalent cations such as B and Al, require charge compensation by oxygen
 atoms (also referred to as non-bridging oxygens or NBOs) and, when present in a glass,
 they commonly occupy network terminal sites. The presence of large amounts of glass
 formers (Si, Al, Zr, and B) in a glass composition ensures a high degree of network
 crosslinking and yields a material that is relatively more corrosion resistant. Conversely,
 the existence of a large cationic content leads to breaks in the glass network and increases
 susceptibility of the glass to water diffusion.

  Regardless of the glass composition and corrosion conditions, dissolution in water starts
 with hydration of the glass surface and the inward diffusion of hydrogenated species
 (H2O, H3O+) into the silicate network; the latter process is coupled with the outward
 diffusion of mobile alkali cations from the network terminal sites [15, 20]. The two
 processes are collectively referred to as ion exchange or solid state inter-diffusion. It is
 thought that water diffusion into the glassy structure requires the presence of large rings
 consisting of at least six silica tetrahedra; these rings are not commonly found in highly
 polymerized glasses. As a result, ion exchange processes are expected to dominate the
 initial phase of glass corrosion. They are also dominant when alteration of the glass takes
 place under acidic or near neutral conditions due to the abundance of H+. Diffusion of
 mobile alkalis from the glass structure raises the pH in the surface microenvironment,
 which, in turn, leads to hydrolysis of the covalent bonds (Si-O-M, where M-Si, Al, Zr,
 Fe, Zn, etc.) of the silicate network. As glass hydrolysis progresses, the [Si] in solution
 increases and saturation with respect to H4SiO4 or other species is reached. Near
 saturation, precipitation, and in situ condensation reactions are known to occur involving
 partially or totally detached silica species.

 The above-described processes are depicted by the following reactions [16]:

 11

 Initial hydrolysis and alkali ion exchange

        ≡Si-O- Na+ + H2O → Si-OH + Na+ + OH-                                                    (2.1)

        ≡Si-O- Na+ + H+ → Si-OH + Na+                                                           (2.2)

 Hydrolysis of the silicate network

        ≡Si-O-M≡ + H2O → ≡Si-OH + HO-M≡                                                         (2.3)

 Condensation and precipitation reactions

        ≡Si-OH + HO-Si≡ → ≡Si-O-Si≡+ H2O                                                        (2.4)

        H4SiO4 + ≡Si-OH → ≡Si-O-Si(OH)3 + H2O                                                   (2.5)

        H4SiO4 + H4SiO4 → 2SiO2 + 4H2O                                                          (2.6)

 These reactions may occur concurrently during glass alteration and with a rate that
 depends on glass composition, temperature, pH, and solution composition.

  Kinetically, glass corrosion follows three distinct stages. In Stage I, also known as the
 initial or forward rate regime, hydrolysis of the silicate network (Reaction 2.3) is the
 dominant process of glass alteration. During this stage, the concentration of elements lost
 to the solution is low and the rate of glass corrosion is fastest (initial or forward rate, r0).
 The glass dissolves congruently and its dissolution depends mainly on the corrosion
 temperature, solution pH, and glass composition. Towards the end of Stage I, there is a
 gradual decline in the dissolution rate of the glass due to increasing concentrations of
 dissolved silica species and the approach towards saturation; this decline has also been
 variously attributed to the formation of passivation layers, as discussed below. During
 deceleration of glass alteration, incongruent dissolution is seen. During Stage II, the
 dissolution rate drops to the residual rate, which is typically orders of magnitude lower
 than the initial rate, r0. The residual rate is thought to be driven primarily by solid state
 inter-diffusion processes as the dissolved silica species reach steady state and only the
 concentrations of the more soluble glass components (Na, B, etc.) continue to increase,
 albeit at a much-reduced rate. Stage III is characterized by precipitation of secondary
 phases (typically zeolites), which, on occasion, lead to resumption of dissolution [21].
 The latter is thought to be due to the formation of crystalline phases that consume Si and
 Al from the pore solution, thus, driving the system away from saturation. Relatively
 corrosive conditions (pH>10 and/or T ~90°C) appear to be required for resumption of
 alteration to take place [22-25]. Not all glasses undergo Stage III and the time to reach

 12

 Stage III clearly varies with glass composition and reaction conditions. Even for those
 glasses that remain at Stage II for long periods of time, no evidence exists to support
 complete cessation of dissolution [16, 20].

 It should be noted that ion exchange reactions are also observed at the very beginning of
 Stage I but are not considered a formal kinetic stage [17]. The same is true for the “rate
 drop” phase observed at the end of Stage I. Inter-diffusion processes are observed at
 every kinetic stage, whereas the “rate drop” regime is thought of as a transitional phase
 between forward and residual rates.

  Glass corrosion reactions are expected to alter the near-surface structure of the glass in
 specific ways. For example, ion exchange reactions deplete the glass surface from
 outward diffusing alkalis (Na, Li, Cs) and give rise to sigmoidal-shaped concentration
 profiles for these elements that indicate a gradual increase towards the nominal level with
 increasing distance from the glass surface [26, 27]. Because the outward diffusion of
 cations is balanced by inward diffusion, an alkali profile that is anticorrelated to H+ ions
 is expected. Although not strictly subject to ion exchange, B is released congruently with
 alkali in the initial stages of glass dissolution, when inter-diffusion processes dominate
 (also see Section 2.3) [16, 20, 28-30]. The hydrolysis of B-O-Si bonds is expected to
 break the silicate network connectivity, form silanol bonds, and create pathways that
 further support the inward diffusion of water and glass hydrolysis. Eventually the
 remaining silicate network undergoes structural reorganization for charge-balancing
 purposes and for minimizing the free energy of the system. Solid state inter-diffusion
 along with B dissolution and silica condensation reactions are expected to enrich the
 glass surface in silica and to give rise to a surface layer, known as hydrated glass or gel
 layer, that maintains the covalent bonds of and structurally resembles the unaltered (or
 pristine) glass. It has been suggested that, as glass alteration progresses and condensation
 reactions become prominent, the gel layer evolves from a highly porous surface region to
 a condensed phase limiting the transfer of aqueous species into the glass and, thus,
 becoming passivating [29]. At any given time, the rate of diffusion of reactive species
 through the hydrated glass will depend on the thickness, porosity and pore geometry of
 the alteration layer. Once diffusion of reactive species decreases significantly and the
 layer becomes passivating, the dissolution rate of the glass becomes diffusion-controlled.
 The passivating nature of the gel layer and its role in controlling the residual rate of glass
 alteration are still subject to some controversy; the emerging consensus is that both
 solution saturation (affinity term) and the growth of a surface layer that acts as a diffusion
 barrier play a role in limiting the dissolution rate of the glass [15, 17]. At the basis of this
 consensus is the idea that long-term glass dissolution involves three distinct glass
 alteration “drivers”: a silica-saturated solution, a surface altered layer, and a well-defined
 boundary between the altered glass layer and the pristine glass.

  Predictive models of the long-term dissolution of silicate phases have been developed
 based on thermodynamic and kinetic considerations [10, 11, 31]. Many of these models
 assume the existence of a local equilibrium between the hydrated glass surface and a

 13

The Catholic University of America FY2016 ILAW Glass Testing for Disposal at IDF

 dissolved silica phase. In one of the earliest models [8, 9], glass dissolution is assumed to
 be driven by the decomposition of an activated surface complex (Transition State Theory
 or TST); the activated complex is formed by the interaction of the glass surface with an
 aqueous species (Reaction 2.7). That model is mathematically expressed by Equation 1.1.
 In Reaction 2.7, the forward and reverse reactions that lead to the formation and
 destruction of the activated complex, Matrix-O-Si(OH)3, are considered rapid, whereas
 the decomposition of the complex to form orthosilicic acid (H4SiO4) is considered to be
 rate-limiting.

 (SiO2)matrix + H2O ↔ Matrix-O-Si(OH)3 → H4SiO4 + Surface-OH (2.7)

 According to Grambow [9], the dissolution rate of a silicate glass in an aqueous solution
 can be calculated according to the following equation:

                                     r  k010 . pHT exp(Ea / RT )[1  (C / Csat )] ,                 (2.8)

 where C is the concentration of H4SiO4(aq) and Csat is the concentration of H4SiO4(aq),
 when the bulk solution is saturated with respect to silica. (All other parameters in
 Equation 2.8 are identical to those of Equation 1.1.) Under dilute conditions, C<<Csat and
 Equation 2.8 can be written as:

                                     r0  k010 . pHT exp(Ea / RT ) ,                                 (2.9)

 which provides the initial or forward glass dissolution rate. Although Equation 2.8
 suggests that dissolution will cease when the concentration of orthosilicic acid in the pore
 solution reaches its solubility limit, a finite long-term alteration rate is expected to exist
 because glasses are thermodynamically unstable materials; their continuous conversion to
 a silica phase is therefore expected to persist. The finite long-term alteration rate of the
 glass is distinct from the ion exchange term, included in Equation 1.1; the residual rate
 associated with Stage II of glass corrosion likely incorporates both effects.

 Models that focus on the effects of the affinity term on glass alteration, such as that in
 Equation 1.1, ignore the formation of gel layers that may act as diffusion barriers to the
 movement of aqueous reactants and products; consequently, there are limitations to such
 models regarding long-term glass corrosion. More recently, models have been developed
 that attempt to address the deficiencies of the TST approach including the Grambow-
 Müller model [32], which assumes the creation of a water diffusion layer that restricts
 transport of Si; and the Glass Reactivity with Allowance for Alteration Layer (GRAAL)
 model [31], which assumes the formation of a passivating reactive interface (PRI) with
 increasing solution saturation.

 14

2.2 Interfacial Dissolution Precipitation - A New Model of Glass Corrosion

 The classical understanding of glass corrosion, centered around the notion of surface
alteration layers as the products of solid state inter-diffusion coupled with glass hydration, has
recently come under scrutiny [16, 33-34]. The source of this scrutiny is a limited number of
studies reporting high resolution structural images and multi-dimensional chemical distribution
maps of altered surface layers that refute the expected sigmoidal concentration profiles for
elements associated with inter-diffusion [30, 34, 35]. To reconcile these findings, alternate
theories of glass dissolution have started to emerge that are bound to be debated in the years to
come. A brief description of the studies supporting the existence of non-classical corrosion
mechanisms is provided below.

 In 2010, Geisler et al. studied the acidic dissolution (1M HCl and 150°C) of a glass with
a composition similar to the German HLW glass (used at the reprocessing plant in Karlsruhe,
Germany) and observed that network modifiers (Na, Li) and B continued to be released at
approximately stoichiometric levels well after the bulk solution became saturated with respect to
amorphous silica [33]. After imaging the cross-sectioned glass, they reported the presence of an
altered surface region of a porous glass consisting of two textually distinct zones: an inner one
exhibiting complex oscillatory banding structures and a simple outer rim comprising almost pure
SiO2. They also observed that the inner zone and the pristine glass were separated by a free space
or gap, which they attributed to the existence of an interfacial solution. Chemical analysis of the
patterned inner zone revealed compositional oscillations of areas rich in Ti and Si and rich in Al
and Ce. The concentrations of mobile elements (Na, B, Li) decreased sharply at the reaction
interface without exhibiting a sigmoidal-shaped profile, typically associated with diffusion.
Time-of-flight secondary ion mass spectrometry (ToF-SIMS) and IR spectroscopy analyses of
glass monoliths corroded either in 18O-enriched or 26Mg-enriched solution indicated an
enrichment of the altered surface zone in both elements, consistent with precipitation of 18O and
26
 Mg from the solution rather than entry through diffusion. Briefly, 18O atoms were found
incorporated in the tetrahedral silicon network of the inner zone rather than being associated with
diffused water molecules, whereas 26Mg enrichment showed three maxima: one at the outer rim
and two within the inner zone. Collectively, the data were interpreted as the outcome of glass
dissolution that proceeds through an interface-coupled dissolution–precipitation (IDP)
mechanism, “where both dissolution and instantaneous precipitation occur within a solution
boundary layer that ingress into the parent phase as the reaction proceeds” [33]. According to
that mechanism, glass alteration begins with the congruent dissolution of the various glass
constituents until saturation of the interface solution with respect to amorphous silica is reached.
Supersaturation of the boundary solution is expected to be reached fairly soon due to the limited
transport of the silica species from the interface environment to the bulk solution through the
altered glass region. Once the boundary solution becomes supersaturated, silica precipitates to
form a Si-enriched rim within the altered glass. Silica precipitation then drives the interface
system far from saturation and glass dissolution resumes. As the boundary solution undergoes
cycles of supersaturation-undersaturation, rims of precipitated silica are formed giving rise to the
characteristic oscillating bands seen in the inner zone of the altered glass.

 15

The Catholic University of America FY2016 ILAW Glass Testing for Disposal at IDF
Vitreous State Laboratory Final Report, VSL-17R3860-1, Rev. 0

 More recently, Hellman et al. [34] reported that a similar mechanism of glass dissolution
was at play when SON68 glass3 was altered at 50°C in deionized water for periods of time
ranging from 4 days up to 7 months. Using a number of advanced atomic-resolution analytical
techniques ((scanning) transmission electron microscopy (S)TEM, Energy Filtered TEM
(EFTEM), electron energy-loss spectroscopy (EELS), atom probe tomography (APT), and
ToF-SIMS), the investigators reported alteration zones of relatively homogenous morphology,
structure, and chemical composition, with depths of 35-40 nm (at 4 days) to 70-90 nm (at seven
months), and having distinct interfaces with the pristine glass. B, Na, Li, and Ca were uniformly
depleted throughout the alteration zone and exhibited atomically sharp chemical interfaces
between the altered and pristine glass. Influx of aqueous species into the altered glass was
surmised from its enrichment in H, which was devoid of a sigmoidal concentration profile. The
altered glass was similarly enriched in Si, Al, and Fe and in a constant manner throughout the
entire depth, whereas a small enrichment in Zr was discerned only at the interface with the
pristine glass. None of the glass constituents examined in the study exhibited concentration
profiles that would indicate dissolution through a diffusion-controlled mechanism and all of the
cations seemed to be released independently of their charge. When diffusion-controlled, the
dissolution of a cation yields a depletion depth that inversely depends on the cationic charge.
Although the investigators did not provide evidence of an interfacial solution (although an
increase in [H+] at the altered/pristine glass interface was seen in the ToF-SIMS chemical maps),
their data refuted the role of solid state inter-diffusion in the creation of the glass alteration zone
and were in line with an IDP-based mechanism.

 To verify the role of IDP as a credible mechanism of alteration for glass SON68, Gin et
al. repeated the dissolution of SON68 glass in deionized water at 50°C, initially performed by
Hellman et al., and analyzed alteration zones formed after six months of treatment using
ToF-SIMS [16]. The data reiterated the presence of very sharp elemental (B, Na, Ca)
composition profiles at the interface of altered/pristine glasses but, according to investigators,
provided no evidence for the existence of an interfacial solution for IDP to take place. In
addition, some (~30%) B retention was detected within the altered glass, which is inconsistent
with an IDP-based mechanism as B is not known to precipitate out of solution. Consequently, the
existence of a mechanism other than inter-diffusion and IDP was proposed, which involves
cation dissolution limited by water accessibility to the pristine glass. The investigators also noted
that the altered/pristine glass interface should be considered a rough surface, formed from B, Na,
Ca, and Li gradients, and that glass alteration far from and close to saturation conditions should
be investigated separately, as they are likely controlled by different mechanisms. Solid state
inter-diffusion appears to be a contributing mechanism in glass dissolution in dilute
environments [30].

 Although rejected by Gin et al. as a credible mechanism of alteration for glass SON68
[16], additional studies involving that glass support the idea that IDP plays a role in the alteration
of SON68. Bouakkaz et al. [36] followed the dissolution of the glass in a synthetic groundwater
relevant to the geological repository at three different temperatures, at glass surface area to

3
 SON68 is the inactive reference analog of the French R7T7 glass.

 16

solution volume (S/V) ratios of 900-1800 cm-1, and for a period of 1-2 years. In one
experimental set, the investigators studied leaching of glass doped with 29Si in non-doped
groundwater, while in another they studied leaching of non-doped glass in 29Si-doped
groundwater. They subsequently analyzed the altered glass zone of the leached glasses using
ToF-SIMs. They reported the presence of 3 distinct layers: a) an outer rim layer consisting of
precipitated phyllosilicates, b) a gel layer with constant Si content, and c) an interface layer
(adjacent to the pristine glass), where compositional gradients existed. A sharp gradient was
observed for B and Cs, whereas broader ones were detected for Na and Li. The interface layer
additionally exhibited a Si isotope profile of increasing concentration in the isotope present in
pristine glass. On the other hand, the gel layer exhibited a constant Si isotopic signature, in
which the Si isotope present in the groundwater was in excess. This was suggestive of diffusion
of silicon species from the contacting solution into the gel layer and instant and continuous
precipitation of silica at the boundary of gel-interface layers. The gel layer was depleted in B (it
contained only 9% of the boron content of the pristine glass) and Cs, while it contained
appreciable but decreasing (towards the outer rim) levels of Na and Li. The investigators
speculated that Na and Li were retained in the gel layer to balance Al present in the gel structure.
Finally, the Si isotopic signature of phyllosilicates was one of intermediate value between the
isotopic signature of the contacting fluid and the isotopic signature of the gel layer, indicating
that secondary phases were formed through precipitation of Si both from the bulk solution and
dissolved Si from the glass.

 A final study worth considering is the recent mathematical model, developed by Wang et
al. [35] to predict the formation of oscillatory compositional zonings within the altered glass.
Once thought to be associated with weathered minerals and archeological glasses, compositional
oscillations have been reported for glasses leached under controlled conditions in bench-scale
tests; these oscillations are now understood to arise from specific dynamic interactions between
the dissolution front of the glass and the contacting solution. Wang et al. described the
oscillations in terms of a self-organizational mechanism, controlled by: a) the concentration ratio
of the dissolved silica and cations, and b) the difference between the rate of glass dissolution and
the rate of diffusional mass-exchange between the boundary layer and the bulk solution. Briefly,
the investigators proposed the following glass alteration mechanism: Glass dissolution begins
with the slow diffusion of cations from the glass into the contacting solution. At this stage, the
dissolution rate is low and glass alteration is dominated by mass exchange; an altered zone is
formed. As the concentration of cations in the interface between altered glass and bulk solution
increases, local pH increases leading to the acceleration of glass dissolution and the
disappearance of the altered zone. When the dissolution rate reaches the same order of
magnitude as the mass exchange rate with the bulk solution, then oscillatory dissolution may
take place. When the dissolution rate becomes greater than the mass exchange rate, then
accumulation of cations occurs at the interface and becomes evident by a sharp increase in their
interfacial concentration. The accumulation of cations at the interface along with a concomitant
increase in the local pH will lead to increased dissolution; precipitation of zeolites may be
observed. In this light, zeolite formation is the result of resumption of dissolution rather than the
cause, and part of the oscillatory behavior of glass alteration. Furthermore, zeolite precipitation is
expected to remove cations from the boundary solution thus further decreasing dissolution.

 17

 The previously described studies collectively challenge our current understanding of
glass dissolution, especially as it relates to long-term nuclear waste durability. For example, they
suggest that the chemistry of the bulk solution is far less important than the chemistry of the
solution immediately in contact with the reactive front of the glass. Consequently, the use of bulk
solution chemistry alone for prediction of long-term dissolution behavior of nuclear waste
glasses could be misleading. In the study of Ref. [33], glass dissolution continued despite the fact
that the solution was saturated with respect to amorphous silica. In the study of Ref. [37], the
long-term dissolution rate of SON68 glass leached in groundwater for >24 years was
approximately 20 times higher than the residual rate of the same glass leached in a solution
saturated with respect to silica and at the same temperature. Such discrepancies also imply that if
glass alteration is controlled by an IDP mechanism, then the glass dissolution in environments
with limited water availability may be far more dynamic than generally thought. On the other
hand, Linard et al. [38] reported a rate drop in the dissolution of a simple borosilicate glass,
altered in deionized water for more than a year, despite the fact that the bulk solution remained
far from saturation. Inconsistencies with the rate law of Equation 1.1 suggest that improved
understanding of the mechanisms of glass dissolution is imperative for predicting the long-term
behavior. One key question is the universality of alternate mechanisms such as IDP under variant
corrosion conditions. It is possible that both solid state diffusion and IDP occur but they
dominate only in specific alteration environments (dilute vs. saturated, high vs. low temperature,
etc.) [16]. Another key question is the relevance of alternate mechanisms in long-term glass
dissolution. Although such studies are limited in number, the presence of an interface with sharp
concentration gradients of B and alkali, reported to be present in the altered zone of glass SON68
leached for >24 years in synthetic groundwater at 90°C [30], imply that IDP-type mechanisms
may control long-term durability of nuclear waste glasses. The fact that long-term weathering of
silicate minerals exhibits the same oscillatory compositions [39, 40] as the ones reported to be
the result of IDP hints of a mechanism that is widely applicable but not appreciated until the
appearance of recent studies on nuclear waste glasses.

2.3 Boron as a Glass Dissolution Indicator

 B is known to participate in the crosslinking of the silicate network in the form of Si-O-B
and B-O-B bridges. It is generally considered to be a glass former and is widely used as a glass
dissolution indicator under the assumptions that it is not retained in the gel layer nor does it
participate in the formation of secondary phases. However, it is becoming increasingly evident
that B dissolution is not representative of silicate network depolymerization and, instead, tracks
the dissolution of the most soluble alkalis (Na, Li, K) for reasons not readily understood [16, 20,
28-30]. Some of these studies describe this type of release as “alkali diffusive behavior” while
others refrain from attaching any diffusive characteristics to the mechanism that releases B from
the glass. Nevertheless, it has been noted repeatedly that the initial dissolution of nuclear waste
glasses is characterized by the immediate and congruent dissolution of B and Na at a rate that is
much faster than that of many other glass constituents. B and Na releases are inversely
proportional to the square root of time, a principal property of diffusion. Geneste et al. attributed

 18

the unusual behavior of boron release to its speciation in the silicate network coupled with
Na/H3O+ ion exchange [28].

 B is known to exist in the silicate network in the form of III- or IV-coordinated atoms.
Whereas IIIB assumes a triangular configuration in the glassy structure, IVB forms tetrahedra
(BO4-) requiring the presence of an alkali (Na, Li) to balance the charge [41-44]. Tetrahedral B
can form reedmergnerite units, in which all corners of the tetrahedron are occupied by Si atoms
(BO4(0B,4Si)), or dandurite units, in which the corners of the tetrahedron are occupied by three
Si and one B atoms (BO4(1B,3Si)). Addition of Na2O into a simple borosilicate glass favors the
formation of tetrahedral B and forces the transition from IIIB to IVB. This is true for Na2O
contents up to mole ratios R=Na2O/B2O3 equal to 0.5. In complex glasses, such as nuclear waste
glasses, a more intricate effect of Na2O addition on B coordination is expected because
additional glass formers are typically present (Al, Zr), which are known to preferentially capture
charge-compensating cations before B. Furthermore, the presence of a substantial amount of Al
or Zr in the glass is expected to have additional effects on the B structural units. For example, Al
diminishes the incidence of reedmergnerite and dandurite units in the silicate network due to the
Al avoidance rule, which forbids the formation of Al-O-B bridges for thermodynamic reasons; in
those glasses, B is expected to preferentially exist in B-O-B units [43]. On the other hand, the
presence of Zr was shown to suppress the number of reedmergnerite units in the glass while
leaving dandurite units unaffected [43]. Lastly, the nature of the charge compensating cations
also plays a role in the final structure of the borosilicate network as lighter cations such as Li
preferentially balance the charge of BO4- units, whereas heavier cations such as Cs favor
stabilization of terminal NBOs [44].

 As discussed above, one of the first steps in glass dissolution is the release of alkalis
(mainly Na and Li) from the glass network through ion exchange with diffusing hydronium ions.
Because Na cations exist within distinct units of the borosilicate structure, different sites can
become protonated through inter-diffusion; these sites possess distinct Na+ substitution energies
and exhibit different basicity and affinity for H+ binding. Geneste et al. [28] determined that
IV
 B-O-BIV sites have the lowest Na+ substitution energy (0.86 eV), whereas IVB-O-Si sites have a
substitution energy (1.86 eV) that is higher than the one exhibited by terminal Si-O- sites
(1.66 eV). Typically, the units with the lowest Na+ substitution energy are the most basic ones
and will be protonated first. When a sodium cation is replaced by a hydronium ion, the latter
rapidly dissociates to form molecular water and a proton. When the sodium cation is charge-
compensating a BO4- site, the replacing H+ is unable to balance the charge and the site is
hydrolyzed to form a IIIB unit (BO3) and a boroxyl or silanoxyl group; the reaction gives rise to
breaks in the silicate network and initiates its depolymerization. It is postulated [28] that the
resulting IIIBO3 units diffuse outwards as boric acid (H3BO3).

                         Na+ + IVB-O-T → H3O+ + IVB-O-T                                       (2.10)

                         H3O+ + IVB-O-T → H2O + IIIB + T-OH                                   (2.11)

 19

 Based on their ease of substitution, Na+ associated with IVB-O-BIV is expected to become
ion exchanged first with H3O+, followed by those associated with terminal NBOs and IVB-O-Si
sites. In light of the above mechanism, the diffusive nature of B release is easily understood. It is
interesting to note here that the increased susceptibility of B-O-B bridges to hydrolysis (as
compared to hydrolysis of Si-O-Si or Si-O-B bridges), was recognized as early as in 1967 by
Low and N. Ramasubramanian [45]. The investigators also speculated that aggregates or islands
of B2O3 exist on the surface of borosilicate glasses that are important for water adsorption on the
glass surface, hydration, and initiation of network depolymerization [45]. These islands
essentially constitute a type of phase separation.

 Phase separation is a well-studied phenomenon that occurs with complex alkali
borosilicate glasses due to metastable immiscibility [44]. In general, a glass melt will undergo
phase separation if the total free energy of the melt is greater than the total free energy of the
corresponding separated phases. Phase separation can assume different forms. For example, it
may occur as droplets of one phase distributed into another (nucleated separation) or as two
interconnected and interpenetrating networks (spinoidal decomposition). Phase separation types
specific to the presence of B, include fluctuations in the structural arrangements in the
short-range order (conversion of IIIB to IVB), in the intermediate-range order (formation of
superstructures such as boroxol rings), and in the long-range order (formation of boron- and
alkali-rich phases) [44]. Thus, phase separation is something of a continuum making it often
difficult to detect unambiguously. Nevertheless, it is possible that phase separation can affect the
glass dissolution behavior.

 An additional complication with using B as an indicator of glass dissolution is its
apparent complexation with Tri(hydroxymethyl)aminomethane (or simply Tris), the buffer of
choice for most of the tests in the SPFT suite [46]. Tris is commonly used as the contacting
solution in glass dissolution experimentation at constant pH because it is stable and generally
inert. Additionally, Tris buffer has a pKa25°C) value of ~8, which makes it suitable for
applications in the slightly basic (7-9) pH region. However, a recent study suggested that an
aqueous solution of 0.04M Tris increased dramatically (by a factor of 8 in the specific tests) B
release from borosilicate glasses during the first few hours of corrosion and was accompanied by
a concomitant increase in Si dissolution, suggesting acceleration of silicate network
depolymerization [46]. The alteration conditions were: S/V<1 cm-1, pH ~7.4 and T=80°C. The
increase in B dissolution was attributed to the ability of the Tris buffer to complex and remove B
atoms from the surface of the glass. The impact of the use of Tris as the contacting fluid on B
dissolution was not investigated beyond the first 2.5 hrs.

 The studies discussed above collectively indicate that B dissolution from nuclear waste
glasses may not be as representative of silicate network depolymerization as is typically assumed
[16, 28]. In fact, with the current state of knowledge, it is rather difficult to assign with certainty
the mechanistic aspect of B release. As a glass former, B is not subject to inter-diffusion per se
although its release appears to be related to Na/H3O+ exchange, at least under dilute conditions.
In saturating environments, the sharp B concentration profiles observed within the interfacial
zone confirm that B is not subject to solid state inter-diffusion but this may merely reflect the

 20

fact that glass dissolution, and not ion exchange, appear to be the dominant mechanism of
elemental release at the glass reactive zone [16, 34]. In that respect, B does reflect glass network
depolymerization. Gin et al. state that: “B behaves like a glass former albeit differently than
other glass formers such as Al or Si.” [30]. Although it is assumed that B neither precipitates nor
is it retained in the altered glass layer, studies exist that suggest that up to 30% of the B amount
in the pristine glass can be found in the gel layer [16]. Gin et al. postulated that B retention in the
alteration zone is associated with the presence of clusters that are relatively stable and resist
dissolution. Thus, while despite these concerns, boron may still be the best available indicator of
glass dissolution, the various caveats associated with its use should be taken into consideration.

 21

 SECTION 3.0
 TEST MATRIX

 The test matrix used in this work, shown in Tables 3a-d, was modeled after similar
studies performed previously [6, 47]. Each table indicates all of the tests performed at a single
temperature (23, 40, 70, or 90°C) and includes glass dissolution experiments under dilute
conditions (variable pH and with zero influent Si concentration ([Si]in=0); six tests per
temperature) and under saturating conditions (pH=9 and [Si]in≠0; six tests per temperature). Each
segment of Tables 3.1a-d pertains to a unique set of experimental conditions starting with the
specific test temperature (col. 1) at which all 12 specimen trains (col. 2) were run (12 trains per
temperature). Other columns in these tables provide:

  Overall test duration (col. 3)
  Intended analytical sampling interval, Dtsam (col. 4)
  Total intended number of samples to be analyzed per specimen, N (col. 5)
  Initial elemental silicon concentration in leaching (influent) solution, [Si]in (col. 6)
  Leachant flow rate, F (col. 7)
  Leaching solution pH (col. 8) and
  Mass of the glass specimen, mg (col. 9).

 Within a given run (temperature), all analytical samples (S) were indexed according to
train number (index 1<i<12) and order of collection (index 1<j<N).

 The duration of testing in Tables 3.1a-d was based on previous SPFT experimentation
[47], which indicated that steady state was reached within 15 days for tests performed at 40, 70,
and 90°C, and within 30 days for tests performed at 23°C. Typically, a minimum of 20 samples
were collected from the tests performed at 40, 70, and 90°C and a minimum of 10 samples were
analyzed per test. A minimum of 10 samples per test were collected and analyzed from the tests
performed at 23°C. The selected influent Si concentrations, [Si]in, were based on the results
obtained from SPFT testing of LAW glass IDF1-B2 [47]. Similarly, flow rates and glass masses
were selected in order to provide a sufficiently large flow rate/surface area ratio, which ensures
that, for tests to be performed under dilute conditions, glass dissolution takes place in sufficiently
undersaturated solutions. A number of previously published studies indicate that the q/S ratio of
≥10-8 m/s that was selected for the present work is sufficiently large to ensure dilute conditions
for trains #1-6 [6, 48].

 It should be noted that two additional tests were performed in order to aid the estimation
of Kg at test temperatures of 70 and 90°C. These tests assessed the effect of lower than initially
examined [Si]in values on glass dissolution. The additional tests at 70 and 90°C are shown in
Tables 3.1c and 3.1d, respectively; the added [Si]in values are highlighted in red. In Table 3.1c,
values of [Si]in in green indicate the target spiked Si value at the specific train, whereas the
corresponding value in black indicates the actual value. As the table shows, influent solutions for

 22

the initial test at 70°C were inadvertently spiked with Si amounts different than the target.
However, the actual spike concentrations were measured so there was no reason to reject the
results from these tests. The only impact was that the lower range of [Si]in was not covered in
these tests. Accordingly, these results were combined with the results of a later experiment in
which tests with supplemental lower [Si]in values were performed.

 SECTION 4.0
 SAMPLE PREPARATION

4.1 Glass Selection

 The composition of the glass (IDF7-E12) selected for SPFT testing is shown in Table 4.1.
A description of the selection process has been provided previously [49]. Briefly, the glass
shown in Table 4.1 is part of a larger on-going initiative aimed at optimizing the LAW and HLW
glass formulations and processing parameters in order to minimize the volume of glass produced
at WTP and to shorten the plant operating time. An important objective of this initiative is to
determine the likely limits of LAW loading across the full range of expected LAW compositions.
LAW glass formulations developed for the WTP fall into two groups: (i) LAW formulations
developed by VSL to support the Bechtel WTP baseline; and (ii) LAW formulations developed
by VSL for the Office of River Protection (ORP) to support enhancements to the original WTP
baseline. Compared to the WTP baseline LAW glasses, the ORP LAW glasses have higher
waste loadings. Since the major waste loading limiting constituents in Hanford LAW are sodium
and sulfur, glass formulation optimization for ORP has been focused on maximizing the
incorporation of these components in the glass. Thus, the ORP LAW glasses contain higher
sodium and sulfur concentrations as compared to the WTP LAW glasses. Higher sodium (or
alkali) loadings were achieved by modifying the glass former additives through changes such as
increasing the concentration of ZrO2 and introduction of SnO2 as a new additive. Similarly,
higher sulfur loadings were achieved by increasing the concentrations of glass former additives
such as CaO and Li2O and introducing V2O5 as a new additive.

 An overview of the Na2O and SO3 loadings for the baseline WTP glasses and the ORP
glasses is given in Figure 4.1. As is evident from Figure 4.1, the ORP glasses provide
considerable increases in waste loadings over the WTP baseline glasses and therefore
considerable potential for reductions in cost and schedule. The glass selected for the present
work, IDF7-E12, is one of the high waste loading ORP glasses. The composition of IDF7-E12 is
identical to that of ORPLE12, which was developed for the vitrification of tank waste AZ-101
[49]. As indicated in Table 4.1, the selected glass contains increased amounts of CaO, Li2O, and
V2O5 to allow for the incorporation of 1.5 wt% SO3. It is recognized that it will be possible to
perform SPFT testing on only a relatively small number of additional glasses to support the IDF
PA. Accordingly, glasses are being selected to provide reasonable coverage of the range of likely
LAW compositions. The IDF7-E12 glass is representative of the high-sulfur end of the range of
those compositions, as evident from Figure 4.1. Further details on the rationale for the selection
of this glass are provided in reference [49].

 24

4.2 Glass Preparation

 The IDF7-E12 glass was prepared from reagent grade or higher purity chemicals
according to VSL standard operating procedures. Briefly, an appropriate combination of
well-mixed chemicals was melted at 1200ºC for 75 minutes in a platinum-gold crucible. Mixing
of the melt was accomplished with a platinum stirrer beginning 15 minutes after the start of
melting and continuing for the next 60 minutes, until the end of melting. The procedure yielded
an approximately 500 g-batch of a clear emerald glass with a small sulfate layer visible at the
surface. The IDF7-E12 glass composition was selected such that it maximizes sulfate loading in
the glass but does not form a separate sulfate layer on the melt surface in melter testing [49].
Since the sulfate concentration in the glass was deliberately selected to be at the maximum
loading allowable based on melter testing, it is not surprising that a small sulfate layer was
visible on the surface of the crucible glass sample. For LAW processing at the WTP, the nominal
glass composition that will be selected will have a lower waste loading (and sulfate loading) such
that expected process variations can be accommodated and, therefore, formation of separate
sulfate phase in the melter is not expected to be an issue. A small fraction of the as-melted
sample was collected for analyses. The balance of the melt was then directly heat-treated
according to the WTP LAW canister centerline cooling (CCC) profile [50]. The sample collected
after CCC treatment exhibited a center area of translucent dark green glass and a region in
contact with the crucible walls that appeared to be opal light green (Figure 4.2). This lighter
colored crystallized section at the crucible contact was easily set apart from the rest of the glass
allowing segregated quantitative evaluations of the two sections. Again, as stated above, since
the nominal composition for processing LAW at the WTP will be selected at a lower waste
loading to accommodate process variations, the observed crystallization is not expected to be an
issue for this application. In addition, any crystallization that occurs at the container interface
will be a very small fraction of the total amount of glass in the container and will not have any
significant effect on ILAW glass product quality as determined by the relevant tests such as PCT.
Furthermore, while the entire crucible is heat treated according to the CCC profile, the actual
LAW container walls will cool much more quickly, which will significantly reduce
crystallization on the walls. Samples obtained from the sections were subject to compositional
and other analyses, as described below.

 The melt batch included a rhenium spike targeted at 0.1 wt% Re2O7, if all of it were
retained in the glass. Since rhenium is subject to significant volatile loss from the glass during
melting, excess Re2O7 was added to compensate, assuming an average retention of 53.5% based
on extensive past testing. This results in the addition of 0.93 g, instead of 0.50 g of Re2O7, to
each batch targeting 500 g of glass.

 Prior to its use in the SPFT tests, the glass was crushed and sieved to a 100-200 mesh
(75-149 m) specification. Ground glass was water-washed and air-dried to remove fines, which
tend to dissolve faster than glass grains during alteration experiments and, thus, interfere with
test results.

 25

The Catholic University of America FY2016 ILAW Glass Testing for Disposal at IDF

4.3 Glass Compositional Analysis

 The primary method used for glass composition analysis was X-ray fluorescence
spectroscopy (XRF) on powdered glass samples. A PANalytical AxiosmAX-Advanced XRF
spectrometer was used for this purpose. The XRF was calibrated over a range of glass
compositions using standard reference materials traceable to the National Institute of Standards
and Technology (NIST), as well as waste glasses such as the Argonne National Laboratory –
Low-Activity Waste Reference Material (ANL-LRM) [51] and the Defense Waste Processing
Facility (DWPF) – Environmental Assessment Glass (DWPF-EA) [52]. Since boron and lithium
are not determined by this procedure, it is supplemented by acid digestion followed by solution
analysis, as described below.

 Glass samples for analysis by direct current plasma atomic emission spectroscopy (DCP-
AES) or inductively coupled plasma atomic emission spectroscopy (ICP-AES) analysis are first
subjected to microwave-assisted total acid dissolution in Teflon vessels according to VSL
standard operating procedures. Twenty milliliters of a 1:5 mixture of concentrated HF:HNO3 is
diluted to 50 ml and used for the dissolution. This procedure is similar to the ASTM Test Method
C1412-99, which also employs a mixture of concentrated HF and HNO3 in microwave digestion
of pulverized glass samples; supplemental use of HCl/H3BO3 is not included in the VSL
procedure since boron is normally one of the analytes. The solutions resulting from the acid
digestion are analyzed by DCP-AES or ICP-AES.

 The glass composition analysis was performed on triplicate samples. Samples are
typically rejected when major oxides, with target concentrations of 1 wt% or more, show relative
deviations from the target composition of greater than 10% (or 0.2 wt% absolute for SO3). Table
4.2 shows the results of the compositional analysis of the “as melted” sample (IDF7-E12-3)
along with the compositional analyses of samples taken from the center (IDF7-E12CCC1-center)
and the crucible-contact (IDF7-E12CCC1-crucible) areas of the CCC-heat treated glass. The data
in Table 4.2 show that the composition of the glass was on target with respect to the major glass
constituents for all analyzed samples. The “as melted” and “CCC1-center” samples exhibited
slightly reduced SO3 values compared to the target composition suggesting some sulfate loss
during melting of the glass, as has been observed previously in preparation of high sulfate LAW
glasses. In contrast, the “CCC1-crucible” sample was much enriched in SO3 but not in any other
glass constituents indicating sulfur accumulation at the crucible walls during CCC heat
treatment. Glass ground from segments of the center area of the crucible (IDF7-E12CCC1-
center) was used for SPFT testing. Consequently, all of the SPFT data presented here were
obtained exclusively from sample IDF7-E12CCC1-center and all references to glass IDF7-E12
from here forward refer to IDF7-E12CCC1-center. This material was crushed, sieved, and
washed prior to use, as described above.

 Further evaluation of the IDF7-E12 glass included scanning electron microscopy (SEM)
imaging of cross-sectioned, carbon-coated and polished grains, and X-ray diffraction (XRD)
analysis. Additional analyses indicated the presence of small amounts (0.1 vol% by SEM and
0.3 wt% by XRD) of zincochromite spinel crystals, which ranged in size from 0.5 to 2 m. SEM

 26

imaging also revealed the presence of spherical voids about 2-3 micron size that were
heterogeneously distributed within the glass. These voids are thought to be the result of the
dissolution (during SEM specimen preparation) of a water-soluble sulfate phase. Analysis by
energy dispersive X-ray spectroscopy (EDS) confirmed that the composition of the glass grains
was constant throughout the glass. Figure 4.3 shows SEM micrographs of IDF7-E12 grains with
spherical zincochromite (A) and spherical voids (B) present. Figure 4.4 shows an XRD pattern
indicating the presence of trace amounts of ZnCr2O4 crystals. Although ZnCr2O4 is barely
detectable by XRD its presence is ascertained from the SEM analysis (Figure 4.3). In Figure 4.4,
peaks at 7.5 and 15º remain unidentified because XRD patterns of crystalline phases supplied
with the software package for identification purposes often lack the low (<15°) 2 angle region.
Most likely the unidentified peaks belong to corundum, used here as an internal standard for
quantification purposes.

 27

The Catholic University of America FY2016 ILAW Glass Testing for Disposal at IDF
Vitreous State Laboratory Final Report, VSL-17R3860-1, Rev. 0

 SECTION 5.0
 SPFT TESTING AND DATA ANALYSIS

5.1 Description of the Experimental Set-up

 The SPFT apparatus used in the present work follows recommendations provided in the
corresponding ASTM standard practice [53] and is functionally equivalent to one previously
employed for other LAW glass SPFT testing [6, 47]. Figure 5.1 shows a single specimen test
train marked by an index “i” along the diagram. There were several such trains running
simultaneously at the same constant temperature for the duration of the given test set (see the test
matrix in Section 3.0). The only wetted materials throughout the system were plastics. These
include high-density polyethylene (HDPE), polypropylene (PP), polyetheretherketone (PEEK),
perfluoroalkoxy polymer (PFA), and fluorinated ethylene propylene co-polymer (FEP). These
materials are known not to leach out any components that are relevant to the glass leaching
process. However, since leaching solutions have elevated pH values and all tests have long
durations, protection from ingress of atmospheric CO2 is an issue, as all plastics are somewhat
permeable to carbon dioxide. To address this, all reservoirs and enclosures were continuously
purged with CO2-free air (<1 ppm CO2 from a dedicated Balston model 75-52NA “zero” gas
generator). Reservoir bottles were outfitted with filters so influent solutions were filtered
constantly to ensure that no silica crystals were present. A short length of interconnecting PEEK
tubing (less than 1 m of 1.59 mm OD x 0.51 mm ID) was exposed to ambient atmosphere – an
insignificant exposure considering PEEK’s low carbon dioxide permeability [51] of 6.1·10-9
cm3(STP)·cm/cm2·s·atm at 25 °C, which is an order of magnitude lower than those for commonly
used FEP and PFA materials4 (7.0·10-8 and 9.4·10-8 cm3(STP)·cm/cm2·s·atm at 25 °C,
respectively).

 The leaching solution was prepared ahead of time in a quantity sufficient for the entire
test duration. The solution was contained in a continuously purged reservoir R from which it is
drawn into a pump P and discharged through the reaction cell (LC). The pump operation was
computer-controlled. Two pumps were used each of which controlled six trains. Upon entering
the constant temperature oven the leaching solution passed through a 50 cm long thermal
equilibration coil (TEC) before reaching the reaction cell. The solution left the reactor and
analytical samples were dispensed downstream via a robotic arm into a series of vials. The
robotic arm was computer-controlled. Samples were collected periodically throughout the tests at
predetermined times.

4
 Data from IDX Health and Science (formerly Upchurch Scientific) 2009-2010 Catalogue, p. 75.

 28

The Catholic University of America FY2016 ILAW Glass Testing for Disposal at IDF
Vitreous State Laboratory Final Report, VSL-17R3860-1, Rev. 0

5.2 Solution Preparation

 Glass leaching experiments at a pHRT5 range from 7 to 10 were performed in 0.05 M
tris(hydroxymethyl)aminomethane (Tris) solutions buffered to the target pH using concentrated
HNO3 or 1 M LiOH as described by Pierce et al. [6]. Glass leaching experiments at pHRT 11 and
12 were performed in solutions of 0.001 M LiOH and 0.01 M LiOH, respectively, together with
0.01M LiCl for consistency with previous tests [6]. The pHT6 values of the buffer solutions,
heated to the test temperature, were measured using a temperature-compensated pH meter.
Determination of pHT was necessary for the calculation of dissolution rates using Equation 1.1.
Table 5.1 shows the pH values obtained at various temperatures.

 Leaching solutions containing varying amounts of Si were formulated by dissolving
analytical grade silicic acid powder (SiO2.H2O) in 0.05 M Tris. Due to the low solubility of
amorphous silica in aqueous solutions, stock solutions containing at least 140 ppm [Si] were
prepared, heated at 90°C for a period of time, decanted carefully into fresh bottles to avoid
transfer of precipitated solids, and analyzed by ICP-AES or DCP in order to determine the
concentration of Si. As necessary, the stock solutions were diluted to the target [Si] and buffered
to pHRT 9 as previously described.

5.3 Sample Analysis

 Collected samples, designated for solution analysis, were acidified with a small amount
of concentrated HNO3 and analyzed by ICP-AES for the presence of B, Al, Si, and Na. Samples
from selected tests were additionally analyzed for Li and Ca (by ICP-AES) and Re and Cs (by
inductively coupled plasma-mass spectroscopy (ICP-MS)). Analysis of Li, Cs, and Ca was
performed in order to assess congruency of dissolution among alkali cations and to potentially
assist in understanding the role of ion exchange in the alteration of IDF7-E12. Rhenium is added
into the formulation of LAW glasses as a non-radioactive surrogate for Tc and its dissolution
behavior therefore provides information regarding the long-term immobilization of Tc in LAW
glasses. Results from the additional analyses are presented in Table A5 of Appendix A. Each test
also included a collection of samples that were designated for pH measurements in order to
ensure that solution pH was maintained constant throughout a specific test.

 A total of 54 tests were performed and >600 individual samples were analyzed for
solution chemistry. A sample was accepted for analysis only if its flow rate deviated from the
average flow rate of its specific train by ≤5%. The average flow rate for all tests was within 10%
of the corresponding target flow rate. At the end of the experiment, the altered glasses were
collected, water-washed gently, and air dried. Altered glass samples were archived. One altered
glass sample and its unaltered counterpart were analyzed by SEM for the presence of phase
separation (see Section 6.2.4).

5
 Subscript indicates pH values obtained from measurements at room temperature (RT).
6
 Subscript indicates pH values measured at the test temperature T.

5.4 Data Analysis

 Calculation of Dissolution Rates. The data analysis follows the guidelines outlined in the
ASTM Standard C 1662-10, which specifies that, if a glass specimen of a known surface area is
contacted by a solution that flows continuously at a known flow rate and at a constant
temperature, the concentration of the i-th soluble glass component in the effluent solution exiting
the reactor vessel can be used to calculate the amount of glass that has been dissolved. The
dissolution rate of the glass is then calculated as:

                                                C  C   SF 

where: Ci - is the steady-state concentration of component i measured in the effluent solution
 Ci° - is the background concentration of component i in the influent solution as measured
 in a blank test
 F - is the solution flow rate
 S° - is the initial surface area of the glass sample that is exposed to solution, and
 fi - is the mass fraction of component i in the glass.

 In Equation 5.1, the surface area (So) is calculated as the product of the specific surface
area and the initial sample mass (mo) used in the test. The specific surface area is approximated
based on the mesh size of the fraction used in the test and the density of the glass. The particles
of glass are presumed to be spherical with diameter (d) equal to the arithmetic average of the
openings of the sieves used to prepare the material. The specific surface area (Sp) of a glass
having a density  is:

 Sp  6 (5.2)

thus,

                                                S o  S p  mo                                                    (5.3)

 During glass dissolution, the mass and surface area of the glass sample are expected to
change over time as material is dissolved. The surface area Sj at time j during a SPFT test is
calculated from the following expression [53]:

 3 1 2
                                                Sj        m0 3 m j 3                                             (5.4)

The Catholic University of America FY2016 ILAW Glass Testing for Disposal at IDF
Vitreous State Laboratory Final Report, VSL-17R3860-1, Rev. 0

where ro is the radius of the glass particle and mj is the sample mass at time j. The parameter mj
is estimated by the equation [54]:

 1  j 1 t j 
                                     mi  m o             Fn C n ,i  t n  F j C j ,i        , j≥1               (5.5)
