where i is an element representative of glass dissolution such as B. Here it should be noted that
Equation 5.5 inherently assumes uniform dissolution during SPFT testing and the absence of any
surface alterations such as cracking.

 The volumetric flow rate (F) is calculated by dividing the mass of solution that is
collected by the time for its collection and its density (which can be assumed to be 1 g/cm3 in
most cases in view of the dilute solutions involved).

 Determination of Steady State. To determine the steady-state concentration (Ci), the
measured concentration of the i-th component is plotted versus time in order to visually identify
the data to be used. Data that precede the attainment of steady-state conditions, essentially
constant concentration of the analytes, are excluded before calculating the mean and standard
deviation of the subsequent data points. The relative standard deviation (the standard deviation
divided by the mean) of the calculated steady state concentration must be less than 0.15 for the
test results to be valid [53]. In the results described in Section 6.0, the relative standard deviation
was ≤0.1. Soluble components monitored in this fashion include silicon, aluminum, sodium, and
boron. Parameters of Equation 1.1 are estimated using normalized boron dissolution rates
(NDR). However, differences in rates calculated using different elements may provide added
insight into the dissolution mechanism. For example, the rate of sodium ion exchange (rIEX) at
steady state is typically calculated by subtracting the boron from the sodium dissolution rate [3].
This value has been used to provide an estimate of the contribution of ion exchange to the overall
leaching of sodium from the glass [3, 6].

 Estimation of Kg. Kg is estimated from the NDR values obtained at steady state when the
glass is leached in the presence of increasing influent concentrations of Si and at specific pH and
T. To determine the parameter Kg at a given T, NDR values are plotted as a function of the
activity of orthosilicic acid (a[H4SiO4]) dissolved in the effluent solutions at steady state
(Equation 1.1) and a linear regression on the data is performed. Values of a[H4SiO4] are
determined from the corresponding solution chemistry using the geochemical code PHREEQC.
According to Equation 1.1, Kg is given by the value of a[H4SiO4] at NDR=0 [6]. To determine
the Kg value at the presumed repository temperature of 15°C, the natural logarithm of the
measured Kg values is plotted against the reciprocal of the absolute temperature and a linear
regression is performed on the data [6].

 Verification of Dilute Conditions - Adjusted NDR. Dilute conditions assume that the
concentration of orthosilicic acid, dissolved during glass alteration, is so low that the system
remains essentially far from saturation and experiences the forward dissolution rate (Q<<K and,

 31

thus, Q/K=0 in Equation 1.1). However, while in practice, the test conditions are selected to
ensure that the orthosilicic acid concentrations are small, they are necessarily non-zero. As a
result, the measured NDR will depart slightly from the forward rate because the affinity term is
slightly less than one. To account for this, the magnitude of the ratio Q/Kg of Equation 1.1 is
calculated and the measured steady state NDR values are divided by the calculated affinity term;
in the present work, such corrections were typically only a few percent, as discussed in Section
6. For this calculation, for an effluent solution far from saturation, Q is the activity of orthosilicic
acid (Q= a[H4SiO4]) in solution and the estimated Kg at the specific temperature is used. These
adjusted NDR values are then used for estimating the parameters , Ea, and k0.

 Calculation of Parameters , Ea, and k0. To determine the dependence of glass
dissolution rate on pH (the parameter  in Equation 1.1), log NDR is plotted as a function of pHT
at a specific temperature and linear regression of the data is performed for pHT >7 or 8. Because
the parameter  is assumed to be independent of temperature, the power law coefficient for a
particular glass is considered to be the average value obtained at various temperatures. Once the
parameter  is determined in this way, the activation energy Ea and rate constant k0 of the glass
are determined by performing a nonlinear regression on all data obtained under dilute conditions
([Si]in=0)) using Equation 1.1 [6].

 Estimation of rIEX and EIEX. The ion exchange rate, rIEX, of the glass at a specific
temperature was obtained from the data collected from the tests performed under saturating
conditions at that temperature. Following the convention of McGrail et al. [3] and Pierce et al.
[6], rIEX values were estimated by subtracting B dissolution rates from the Na dissolution rates.
Because B is assumed to be released only through glass dissolution, while Na is released through
a combination of glass dissolution and ion exchange, the results of the subtraction are expected
to represent only the ion exchange term. The results are expressed in mol m-2 s-1. The activation
energy of the Na ion exchange reaction, EIEX, and the rate of ion exchange at 15ºC, are calculated
by plotting the natural logarithm of rIEX as a function of the reciprocal of the absolute
temperature (Arrhenius equation) and performing a linear regression on the data.

 32

The Catholic University of America FY2016 ILAW Glass Testing for Disposal at IDF
Vitreous State Laboratory Final Report, VSL-17R3860-1, Rev. 0

 SECTION 6.0
 RESULTS AND DISCUSSION

 The data obtained from elemental analysis of collected effluent samples are listed in
Appendix A along with the corresponding normalized7 dissolution rates for each analyzed
element as calculated from Equation 5.1; these transient rates then converge to the steady state
dissolution rates once steady state is reached. Thus, the normalized dissolution rates for each
element (NDRi) were plotted against time and steady state was determined for each analyzed
element for each set of experimental conditions. Samples used to determine steady state for a
specific set of experimental conditions are shown in bold in Tables A1- A4 in Appendix A.
Steady state B dissolution rates (mean±SD) are further highlighted in Table A6 of Appendix A
along with the coefficient of variance. Table A6 features unadjusted rates as determined from the
highlighted samples of Tables A1-A4. These graphs are provided in Appendix B, where they are
organized by test temperature and for dilute or saturating conditions. The y-axes of the graphs in
Appendix B show NDRi, where i is one of the following elements: B, Al, Na, Si, Li, Ca, Cs, or
Re. In Appendix B, pages B11 and B12 provide summary graphs of B and Si dissolution rates,
respectively, for all tests performed under saturating conditions as a function of the activity of
the silicic acid in solution; those graphs are furnished with error bars representing standard
deviations in order to qualify dissolution trends. In the text below, when the term “dissolution
rate” is used, it refers to normalized values. It should be noted that the majority of the graphs
shown in the sections below (main report) are constructed from steady-state dissolution rates,
which are also referred to as NDRi; the only exceptions are for plots of NDRi vs. time, which,
obviously, also show non-steady state dissolutions rates. The glass dissolution rate is denoted as
NDR; since boron is used as the preferred indicator of glass dissolution, this term refers to the
dissolution rate of B (NDRB) at steady state.

6.1 Dynamic Dissolution of IDF7-E12 Under Dilute Conditions

 6.1.1 Congruency of Dissolution and Mechanistic Considerations

 Data and graphs relevant to dissolution of IDF7-E12 at various pH and temperature
values under dilute conditions are shown in Appendix B, pages B1-B4. The data suggest that
alteration of IDF7-E12 far from saturation follows the classical understanding of glass
dissolution. In acidic environments (pHRT<7, see Table 5.1), glass alteration is dominated by ion
exchange phenomena: the release rate of monovalent cations such as Na+ and Li+ far exceeds that
of Si. As expected from published reports [16, 20, 28-30], B dissolution tracks that of Na and Li
through hydronium-driven hydrolysis of B-O bonds (Reactions 2.11 and 2.12). Very little Si or
Al release is observed. In slightly alkaline environments, where solid state inter-diffusion is

7
 Normalized with respect to the content of the element i in the glass composition.

 33

The Catholic University of America FY2016 ILAW Glass Testing for Disposal at IDF
Vitreous State Laboratory Final Report, VSL-17R3860-1, Rev. 0

expected to diminish, and silicate network depolymerization reactions are expected to begin to
dominate, glass constituents dissolve congruently with the exception of Na, which shows
increased dissolution when the solution buffering system is LiOH-based (pHRT 11 or 12). In
those instances, the inward diffusion of Li+ and its exchange with Na+ results in increased Na
release (see Appendix B, graphs at pHRT 11 or 12). Because Li+ is able to charge compensate
tetrahedral B, replacement of Na+ by Li+ at BO4- sites does not lead to hydrolysis of B-O bonds.
Consequently, at pHRT 11 or 12, B dissolution does not track that of Na. The presence of Li is
expected to suppress the diffusive nature of B dissolution and make it a better indicator for
network dissolution. When analyzed, Ca dissolution rates were slightly lower than those of Na
and Li. This is commonly observed with dissolution of nuclear waste glasses. Tournié et al., who
investigated the role of CaO on the dissolution of simple glasses, suggested that Ca++ is either ion
exchanged with H3O+ very slowly or exchanged and immediately trapped in the altered glass
layer [46]. Because the presence of CaO in silicate glasses has been shown to decrease glass
dissolution, the investigators postulated that Ca++ trapped in the gel layer assists in the
cross-linking of the newly re-organized silicate network, thus, increasing the density of the gel
layer and reducing the inward diffusion of H2O. However, a third explanation may be plausible.
In complex borosilicate glasses, calcium cations function to preferentially charge compensate
glass formers such as Al and Zr while avoiding boron anionic clusters [43]. Because alkalis near
IV
 B-O-BIV sites are the first to be ion-exchanged, followed by alkalis associated with network
terminal NBOs and Si-O-BIV [28], the difference in the release rates between Na and Ca may
also reflect the basicity of the various anionic sites in the glass network and the preferred type of
cation for charge compensation. Finally, a common theme throughout the graphs of Appendix B,
pages B1-B4, is that Al dissolution is controlled by the aqueous solubility of Al: at neutral or
near-neutral pH, where Al3+ is scarcely soluble, the Al dissolution rate is very low, whereas, at
highly alkaline pH, where Al3+ is soluble, Al dissolution rates that exceed those of Si or B are
observed.

 6.1.2 Estimation of Kinetic Parameters , Ea, and k0

 To calculate the kinetic parameters , Ea, and k0, the steady state glass dissolution rates
were adjusted to account for the fact that Q/Kg is not precisely zero, as described in Section 5.4.
Table 6.1 shows the difference between measured glass dissolution rates (rm) and the adjusted
dissolution rates (r0). With the exception of two experimental sets (pH 8 at 90 and 70°C), the
difference between rm and r0 is ≤8%8 and, in fact, the overwhelming majority of adjusted rates
differ from the measured values by ≤2% (see Table 6.1), indicating that the system remains far
from saturation throughout the various tests. The adjusted dissolution rates are designated as r0
because they are considered equivalent to the forward dissolution rate of the glass at a specific
set of experimental conditions.

8
 Because the experimentally determined Kg(90°C) value was an outlier, the projected Kg value from Figure 6.10
was used to adjust the dissolution rate values at 90°C. See also the comment in Section 6.2.3.

 34

 Based on the r0 values reported in Table 6.1, the power-law coefficient , activation
energy Ea, and rate constant k0 were calculated as described in Section 5.4. Figure 6.1 shows the
plots of log NDR vs. pHT used to determine the  value at each temperature. Table 6.2
summarizes all of the obtained estimates from Figure 6.1. Because the parameter  is expected to
be independent of temperature, its value for IDF7-E12 is obtained by taking the mean of the
values at each temperature, which gives a value of 0.32±0.03. This value is close to the one
reported for glass LAWB45 (0.34) but somewhat lower than the corresponding values of other
LAW glasses (LAWA44: 0.49; LAWC22: 0.42; IDF1-B2: 0.48) [6, 47, 55]. Reported values for
HLW glasses are: 0.39 for the French glass R7T7, 0.43 for British Magnox glass, and 0.38-0.49
for Magnox-THORP blend glasses [48]. Chave et al. reported  values of 0.49 and 0.33 for the
silicate mineral obsidian and an archeological glass, respectively [20].

 A non-linear regression, performed on all data obtained for glass IDF7-E12 at pHT >7,
gave an apparent Ea value of 55±5 kJ mol-1 and a kinetic rate constant of k0=5.7 x 104 g m-2 d-1;
the data and regression results are plotted in Figure 6.2. The activation energy of IDF7-E12
appears to be within the expected range for the activation energy of silicate dissolution, which
varies between 50 and 90 kJ/mol [56]. This further supports the idea that the breakage of a Si-O
bond is the rate-limiting step in the alteration of IDF7-E12 under dilute conditions. For
comparison purposes, the activation energies of other glasses and silicate minerals are: quartz
(66-83 kJ mol-1), amorphous silica (74.5 kJ mol-1), basaltic glasses (72.4 kJ mol-1), French glass
R7T7 (71 kJ mol-1), LAWA44 (60 kJ mol-1), LAWB45 (53 kJ mol-1), LAWC22 (64 kJ mol-1),
and IDF1-B2 (64 kJ mol-1) [47, 55, 56]. Finally, the kinetic constant k0 of the IDF7-E12
dissolution is in the general range (~104 g m-2 d-1) seen with other LAW glasses [47, 55].

6.2 Dynamic Dissolution of IDF7-E12 Under Saturating Conditions

 6.2.1 Congruency of Dissolution and Mechanistic Considerations for Tests
 Performed at 70 and 90°C

 Data and graphs relevant to dissolution of IDF7-E12 under saturating conditions at 90
and 70°C are shown in Appendix B, pages B7-B10. An examination of the dissolution trends
exhibited in the graphs for the 90°C test (pages B9-B10) leads to the following observations:

  When the influent solution was spiked with 1 ppm Si, the majority of the analyzed
 elements (B, Na, Si, and Re) were leached congruently and at a dissolution rate that was
 above the forward rate (r0=0.1585 g m-2 d-1; see Table 6.1). According to the classical
 understanding of glass dissolution, the forward rate is the maximal rate of dissolution
 exhibited by a specific glass composition under a specific set of environmental
 conditions. Interestingly, the dissolution rate of Cs was very close to r0, whereas the
 dissolution rate of Al was above that of all other elements.
  When the influent solution was spiked with 5 ppm Si, all elements dissolved congruently
 at steady state. However, at Day 1 of alteration, dissolution rates (~0.3-0.4 g m-2 d-1) well

 35

 above the forward rate were recorded for all elements. These rates slowly decreased
 towards steady state. This phenomenon has often been attributed to the initial dissolution
 of fines and rough surfaces, which is faster than that of the bulk glass. Complexation of
 elements with Tris and increased dissolution may also contribute [46]. However, this
 does not appear to be an entirely satisfactory explanation as the phenomenon seems to be
 more pronounced with influent solutions of intermediate levels of saturation (see other
 graphs in Appendix B, pages B9-B10).
  When the influent solution was spiked with 10 or 15 ppm Si, dissolution incongruences
 become apparent at steady state: initially with Si (10 ppm) and then with Si and Re
 (15 ppm). In both instances, Si dissolves faster than all other elements, whereas the
 dissolution of Re decreases with 15 ppm [Si]in, compared to other elements but also
 compared to the forward dissolution rate of the glass at pH 9 and 90°C.
  When the influent solution was spiked with 20 or 40 ppm Si, elements such as B, Na, and
 Cs dissolve congruently at steady state, whereas Si and especially Re dissolve at lower
 rates. Interestingly, Si appears to dissolve at a constant rate throughout the test without
 exhibiting a large initial dissolution rate. This observation calls into question the idea that
 the large initial dissolution rates, observed with certain elements in specific tests, are the
 product of dissolution of rough surfaces. Rather, it suggests that they are associated with
 a specific mechanism and their occurrence depends on the experimental conditions.
  When the influent solution was spiked with 80 or 100 ppm Si, all elements with the
 exception of Re appear to dissolve at a constant dissolution rate throughout the
 experiment. The dissolution rate of Re decays from an initial value that is close to the
 forward rate to a steady state value that is 10-fold lower than the NDR value at the
 corresponding experimental conditions. In addition, Cs dissolution appears to lag behind
 that of B, Na, and Si, which dissolve mostly congruently.
  When the influent solution was spiked with 120 ppm Si, the dissolution profile of the
 various elements resembles that of the one seen at [Si]in=80 or 100 ppm with the
 exception of the dissolution of silica species, which is greatly diminished.
  Very little Al dissolution is observed when [Si]in≥40 ppm.

 At 70°C, the dissolution profiles of various elements, altered in solutions with varying
degrees of saturation (low: [Si]in=1ppm; intermediate: [Si]in=5-40 ppm; high: [Si]in≥75 ppm),
generally resembled the corresponding dissolution profiles at 90°C and for that reason they are
not discussed in detail here. A common theme between the two tests that is worth examining is
the fluctuations of the steady state silicon dissolution rates with increasing Si influent
concentrations, shown here in Tables 6.3 and 6.4 for tests performed at 70 and 90°C,
respectively. The tables suggest that the dissolution of silicon exhibits oscillations of increasing
and decreasing rates as the solution becomes increasingly more saturating with respect to
amorphous silica or another silica phase. Similar cycles may exist in a near concomitant manner
with the dissolution of elements such as B and possibly Re (see values in red in Table 6.4). Table
6.4 indicates that NDRRe decays sharply with increasing solution saturation and reaches a

 36

The Catholic University of America FY2016 ILAW Glass Testing for Disposal at IDF

minimum at [Si]in of ~20 ppm; that minimum represents ~5% of the NDRRe value at 1 ppm. A
minor recovery of the NDRRe is seen with [Si]in beyond 20 ppm. In contrast, B dissolution rates
experience only moderate decreases and, for several influent solutions, NDR values remained at
or above the forward dissolution rates of r0=0.1585 g m-2 d-1 (90°C; Table 6.1) or r0=0.0848
g m-2 d-1 (70°C; Table 6.1). The above trends are best illustrated in plots of NDRi vs. [H4SiO4],
shown here in Figures 6.3 and 6.4 for 90 and 70°C, respectively. Figure 6.3 shows that, after an
initial decrease in the dissolution rates of B, Na, and Cs, the rates increase with increasing
concentrations of orthosilicic acid in the solution. There is an apparent inversion point in the
profiles of these elements that occurs at [Si]in=20 ppm. The same is true for B and Na dissolution
profiles at 70°C (Figure 6.4); at that temperature, the inversion in the direction of the plot is seen
at [Si]in=50 ppm. It is clear that the above observations are at odds with the classical
understanding of glass dissolution, which anticipates decreased glass dissolution rates with
increasing solution saturation under all experimental conditions.

 Another common theme among the plots in Appendix B, pages B7-B10 is the existence
of dissolution profiles exhibiting a large initial dissolution rate followed by a steady decay to a
constant rate. These profiles are associated with certain elements and certain degrees of solution
saturation and, thus, appear to be associated with a specific mechanism. Whereas elements such
as B and Na appear to follow this type of dissolution path at intermediate levels of saturation, Re
dissolution proceeds exclusively through such a profile at all [Si]in values except for 1 ppm.
Consequently, it is of interest to investigate the mechanisms of glass alteration that yield this
type of dissolution behavior. One explanation is that this behavior arises from an initial large
release of the glass constituent (due to dissolution of fines and/or rough surfaces upon first
contact with water) followed by the gradual replacement of the effluent solution without the
concomitant dissolution of the constituent or with dissolution at a lower rate than the initial rate.
This is a classic continuously-stirred reactor tank (CSRT) problem. In the extreme limiting case
where there is an initial instantaneous release of the glass constituent and the subsequent
dissolution rate is zero then the concentration of the constituent in the effluent solution is
expected to follow the equation:

 f
                                        Ci ,t  Ci ,0 exp(     t)   ,                               (6.1)
 V

where Ci,t is the effluent concentration of constituent i at time t, Ci,0 is the initial concentration of
constituent i in solution, f is the flow rate and V is the reactor volume. The ratio f/V determines
the residence time of the constituent in the CSRT and the exponential decay simply reflects the
flushing out of the initially released constituent. To illustrate this, effluent concentrations of B,
Na, Si, and Re associated with a single test ([Si]in=40 ppm; pH 9/90°C) were fitted to Equation
6.1 and the results are depicted in Figure 6.5. Not unexpectedly, most of the analytes do not
follow the extreme CSRT behavior. However, rhenium concentrations do roughly follow that
behavior, providing some support to the idea of a rapid initial release followed by much lower
subsequent rate of release for rhenium, though the reason for this remains unclear.

 The dissolution behaviors depicted in Figures 6.3 and 6.4 have been described before and

 37

commonly attributed to glass alteration controlled by an activated complex of unknown and
possibly evolving nature [3, 47, 57, 58]. This explanation satisfies the principles of glass
dissolution, as classically understood. Yet, no investigator has been able to successfully describe
such a complex. On the other hand, glass dissolution, which increases with increasing
concentrations of orthosilicic acid in the bulk solution (as in the case of Figure 6.3 for
[Si]in>20 ppm), is not prohibited by the IDP mechanistic model, which asserts that glass
dissolution is driven by the saturation of the interfacial solution rather than the saturation of the
bulk solution. We postulate here that, at the initial stages of IDF7-E12 dissolution (low saturation
level), simple silicate depolymerization controls element release and results in congruent
dissolution, as in the case of tests performed with [Si]in=1 ppm. At this stage, no alteration layer
is formed and, thus, dissolution is controlled by the bulk solution. Increasing amounts of influent
silicon assists in the formation of a hydrated glass zone through initial condensation reactions on
the glass surface. With the onset of an alteration zone, glass dissolves as diffusing water
ingresses into the pristine glass; glass dissolution is controlled by reactions taking place at the
interface between pristine glass and gel layer, thus, becoming independent of the bulk solution
chemistry. With increasing saturation of the interfacial zone, glass dissolution decreases (as in
the case of [Si]in=5-20 ppm and pH 9/90°C). When the interfacial zone becomes supersaturated
with respect to a simple silicon-containing phase, that phase precipitates out forming a rim
around the pristine glass. This undersaturates the interfacial solution and glass dissolution
resumes (as in the case of tests with [Si]in>20 ppm, pH 9/90°C). The rims, formed through this
process, are sufficiently porous to allow the outward transport of elements such as B, Na, and Si
from the interfacial zone to the bulk solution. However, Re, which exists as perrhenate groups
(Re2O7) trapped within the glassy structure, is too large to diffuse out and its dissolution rate
slows down. Glass structural studies indicate that Re is weakly bound to the silicate network and
present primarily as isolated charge-compensated perrhenate tetrahedra, such that its release
requires network dissolution [59, 60]. Furthermore, Gin et al. have shown that the alteration layer
formed from the hydrolysis of the glass acts as a molecular sieve [29]. If the above hypothesis is
correct then high resolution structural imaging should localize Re close to the silica rims formed
through IDP. This could be important because it is expected that Tc dissolution behavior will be
similar to that of Re, which could imply that Tc may remain retained in the alteration layers as
the glass dissolution continues.

 6.2.2 Congruency of Dissolution and Mechanistic Considerations for Tests
 Performed at 23 and 40°C

 Data and graphs relevant to dissolution of IDF7-E12 under saturating conditions at 23
and 40°C are shown in Appendix B, pages B5-B6.

 For dissolution at 40°C, the initial release rate of the various elements corresponds to the
forward rate (r0=0.015 g m-2 d-1) for [Si]in up to 30 ppm and steadily decreases to a steady state
rate; as expected, steady state glass dissolution rates decrease with increasing solution saturation
with the exception of the test performed at [Si]in=15 ppm. In that instance, the release rate of the
various glass constituents remained above the forward rate throughout the duration of the

 38

experiment, yielding an outlier point (Figure 6.6). We have no explanation for this behavior,
especially since no tests were performed at influent Si concentrations between 1 and 15 ppm. It
is plausible that, if an IDP-like mechanism is also at play here, there is an inversion point in the
glass dissolution between these two environments ([Si]in=1 ppm/pH 9/40°C and [Si]in=15
ppm/pH 9/90°C), formed through an IDP mechanism.

 IDF4-E12 dissolution at 23°C mirrored the dissolution at 40°C, with the exception that
no outlier points were observed. For the majority of the tests at this temperature and with the
exception of Re, the initial release of the various glass constituents was at or slightly above the
forward rate (r0=0.0032 g m-2 d-1). High initial release rates for all elements decayed to steady
state dissolution rates that became increasingly suppressed with increasing bulk solution
saturation. For Re, an unusually large initial dissolution rate was observed (4-fold increase
compared to r0) at all [Si]in levels. The reason behind this phenomenon is not apparent. It is
possible that Re is preferentially complexed by Tris and removed from the surface of ground
grains in the first few hours following the contact with water. Re is known to complex with
ethylenediaminetetraacetic acid (EDTA) so its complexation by Tris is not unreasonable [61].
The phenomenon may be exacerbated by the mode of immobilization of Re in the silicate
structure. Nevertheless, steady state dissolution rates of Re were similar to those recorded for
other elements, including B and Na. Figure 6.7 shows plots of dissolution rates of various
elements as a function of the activity of orthosilicic acid in the bulk solution. Figure 6.7 (for
temperature 23°C) along with the corresponding figure at 40°C (Figure 6.6) suggest that
alteration of IDF7-E12 at low temperatures follows the principles of the classical understanding
of glass dissolution. As expected, the graphs in the above figures exhibit linear regions (areas in
the graph where the glass dissolution rate decreases linearly with increasing [H4SiO4]) as well
as regions where the glass dissolution becomes invariant with [H4SiO4], usually because the
glass dissolves at the residual rate.

 6.2.3 Estimation of Parameters Kg and rIEX at 15°C

 As described in Section 5.4, the Kg value of the glass at a specific temperature was
calculated by performing a linear regression on the data points of the linear portion of the NDR
vs. [H4SiO4] plot. The linear regressions performed at each temperature and the resulting Kg
values are shown in the plots of Figure 6.8 for all temperatures. Despite the fact that nine tests
with different amounts of spiked silicon in the influent solutions were performed at 90°C, only
three data points were obtained that appear to have a linear relationship with one another. This is
illustrated in Figure 6.9. The three “linear” data points used for calculating the Kg value at 90°C
(see Figure 6.8D), are highlighted. As previously stated and shown in Table 6.4, very low and
very high amounts of silicon in the bulk solution seem to increase the dissolution rate of the glass
above the forward rate, r0 (Figure 6.9).

 To estimate the value of Kg for IDF7-E12 at the presumed repository temperature of
15°C, ln Kg values previously obtained at 23, 40, and 70°C were plotted as a function of 1/T
(K-1) and a linear regression was performed on the data (Figure 6.10). The Kg value obtained at

 39

90°C was excluded from the linear regression since it was shown to be an outlier. The Kg value
at 15°C was estimated to be 1.87x10-3 mol L-1. This value is in good agreement with the
corresponding values for other LAW glasses [47, 55] (see also Section 6.3).

 Ion exchange rates for all of the tests performed (under dilute as well as under saturating
conditions) were estimated following the McGrail convention (see Section 5.4) and shown here
in Tables 6.5-6.8. According to the classical understanding of glass dissolution and as previously
discussed, ion exchange is expected to dominate at the initial stages of alteration (especially at
low temperature and acidic to neutral pH environments) and, again, when the glass dissolves
near saturation at its residual rate. Consequently, the ion exchange rate of a glass at a specific
temperature is obtained from tests at saturating or near saturation conditions. In Tables 6.5-6.8,
from the tests performed with increasing influent Si concentrations, only tests at 23 or 70°C gave
measurable rIEX values. At 40 or 90°C, B dissolution rates were higher than those of Na and no
ion exchange rates can be reported according to the McGrail convention. At this point, we do not
have an explanation why Na dissolution rates are higher than B dissolution rates at certain
temperatures but not at all temperatures. Recent findings support the idea that, although not
strictly the subject of ion exchange, B is often released congruently with Na by a mechanism that
is secondary to Na+/H+ inter-diffusion. Thus, the McGrail convention very likely underestimates
ion exchange rates. Considering that: a) tetrahedral B undergoes dissolution when the Na cation
responsible for charge compensating the BO4- site undergoes exchange with a proton and b) Na
cations associated with IVB-O-BIV clusters are exchanged before network terminal NBOs, it is
possible that the difference between Na and B dissolution rates merely reflects the number of
terminal NBOs sites involved in inter-diffusion.

 Because no ion exchange rates can be established for two of the four experimental
temperatures tested, it is not possible to determine the activation energy (EIEX) of the ion
exchange reaction or to estimate the rIEX value at the presumed repository temperature (15°C).
Nevertheless, Tables 6.5-6.8 suggest that the ion exchange at 15°C will be less than or equal to
the rate seen at 23°C, or ≤10-10 mol m-2 s-1.

 6.2.4 Phase Separation

 Liquid-liquid phase separation is known to occur in many glass systems. In alkali
borosilicate systems this often involves separation of a boron rich phase from a silicon rich phase
in the melt, which leads to “glass-in-glass” phase separation in the solid. Such phase separation
could lead to more complex dissolution behavior than would be expected based on the simple
rate law of Equation 1.1. As noted in Section 4.3, the glass used for SPFT testing was taken from
the core of the heat-treated sample. It was therefore free of the lazurite crystals induced at the
crucible contact and only trace amounts of zincochromite spinel crystals were present in the
sample. However, to assess the possibility of other phase separation having a role in the
dissolution behavior for IDF7-E12, unaltered (pristine) and altered (alteration conditions:
[Si]in=120 ppm; pH 9; 90°C) IDF7-E12 grains were etched in 5% HCl for 30 sec and imaged by
SEM. Figure 6.11 shows low and high magnification micrographs of unaltered/unetched glass

 40

(Images A and B), unaltered/etched glass (Images C and D), altered/unetched glass (Images E
and F), and altered/etched glass (Images G and H). Features such as spinel crystals
(zincochromite) and spherical voids, visible in Figure 4.3, are also observed in the images of
Figure 6.11. In addition, crystals of lazurite were detected. Lazurite is a sodium sulfate
aluminosilicate mineral (Na8Al6Si6O24S2.5(H2O)0.6), primarily formed in the crucible melt areas
in contact with the Pt walls. Alteration of the glass in [Si]in=120 ppm at pH 9 and 90°C resulted
in the formation of a gel layer that was enriched in Si and depleted in Na. However, the most
interesting feature in the images of Figure 6.11 is the formation of tunnels of variant morphology
(parallel, stepping, or dendritic) after etching of both pristine and altered glass in 5% HCl
(Images D and H). Since SEM-EDS analysis did not reveal any composition variations across the
unaltered/unetched grains, these tunnels imply the existence of a phase that is chemically very
similar to the composition of the pristine glass and whose presence becomes evident only after
acidic dissolution. However, since the images of the altered/unetched glass grains reveal no such
tunnels, it does not appear likely that phase separation is responsible for the unusual dissolution
profile of IDF7-E12 at 90 or 70°C, shown here in Figures 6.3 and 6.4, respectively. This phase
showed signs of preferential leaching in HCl but apparently not in the SPFT leachants. However,
the measured SPFT leachate concentrations include the effects of any preferential leaching of
this phase. Furthermore, if phase separation was responsible, then its effect would be expected to
be seen across all experimental temperatures and not only at 90 or 70°C.

 It is worth noting that there is no WTP requirement that prohibits phase separation in the
ILAW product. Furthermore, the IDF7-E12 glass meets all of the WTP product quality and
processability requirements and exhibits rate law parameter values that are comparable to those
for other LAW glasses that have been tested, as discussed in Section 6.3.

6.3 Summary of Kinetic Parameters for IDF7-E12-Comparison with Other LAW
 Glasses

 A summary of the kinetic parameters of LAW glass IDF7-E12 determined in the present
study is shown in Table 6.9. It is apparent that the glass exhibits kinetic parameters that are
within the expected range for dissolution of silicate minerals and very close to values reported
for other LAW Glasses (Table 6.9). The kinetic parameters of Equation 1.1 (k0, , Ea, and rIEX)
are considered to be intrinsic to a specific formulation and, thus, a dependence on glass
composition is expected. A survey of the compositions of the glasses listed in Table 6.9, shown
in Table 6.10, suggests that, for certain glasses (LAWA44, LAWB45, LAWC22, and IDF1-B2),
the value of  increases with increasing wt% alkalis (Na2O+Li2O+K2O) present in the glass
(Figure 6.12). In contrast, both IDF7-E12 and LAWBP1 exhibit lower  values than would be
anticipated based on the correlation of Figure 6.12, suggesting a more complex compositional
dependence. In terms of Na2O and Li2O content, glass IDF7-E12 is closest to LAWC22.
However, the parameters  and Ea for IDF7-E12 are closest to those of LAWB45. Clearly, the
rate law parameters for more LAW glasses will have to be measured in order to be able to better
discern the compositional dependences of the various kinetic parameters. In terms of rIEX, the
IDF7-E12 value of <10-10 mol m-2 s-1 is well within the range of values exhibited by glasses with

 41

elevated Na2O content.

 Finally, the rate constant k0 is also thought to be an intrinsic property of the glass. In
Table 6.9, with the exception of glass LAWBP1, all other glasses have k0 values on the order of
104 g m-2 d-1. This suggests that the glasses exhibit similar forward dissolution rates upon
initiation of alteration at a given set of experimental conditions. For example, the forward
dissolution rates of all glasses in Table 6.9 except LAWBP1 have values in the range of
(3-9)x10-4 g m-2 d-1 for pHT 7 and 15°C. This suggests that the forward dissolution rates are
relatively insensitive to the presently examined range of composition variations of LAW glasses.
However, in view of the still relatively small data set, this will have to be verified through the
examination of additional glasses.

 42

 SECTION 7.0
 SUMMARY AND CONCLUSIONS

 A complete suite of SPFT tests was performed on the high waste loading LAW glass
IDF7-E12. The results allowed for the determination of the kinetic parameters in the chemical
affinity-based kinetic rate law of Equation 1.1 that is used to model LAW glass dissolution in the
Hanford IDF. All of the kinetic parameter estimates for IDF7-E12 were found to be within the
expected range for dissolution of glasses and silicate phases. These results further confirm results
from the first tests conducted on high waste loading LAW glasses [47], which indicated that the
rate law parameters for those glasses are comparable to those for the baseline WTP glasses. The
results from the present work for the high waste loading glass IDF7-E12 are consistent with that
finding. Despite that consistency, significant deviations from the glass dissolution behavior
expected based on the kinetic rate law of Equation 1.1 were evident in the data for IDF7-E12, as
summarized below.

 A detailed examination of the solution chemistry of the collected SPFT effluent samples
revealed that alteration of IDF7-E12 far from saturation followed closely the classical theory of
glass dissolution (based on solid state inter-diffusion coupled with silicate network hydrolysis
and the formation of a hydrated glass layer that may become passivating over time). In contrast,
alteration of IDF7-E12 in environments of increasing saturation exhibited deviations from the
classical theory of glass dissolution, which requires that the rate of glass dissolution drop as the
bulk solution becomes progressively more saturated with respect to a simple silicate phase, at
least for elevated temperatures (90 and 70°C). Some of the features of the dissolution behavior of
IDF7-E12 at 70/90°C and in gradually saturating solutions are suggestive of an alteration process
that follows the principles of IDP, which proposes that alteration proceeds via congruent
dissolution of the glass into the interface between the pristine phase and the alteration zone and
involves oscillating cycles of silica precipitation and dissolution. If so, this is an important
observation that challenges the kinetic rate law as written in Equation 1.1. One of the most
striking deviations from the kinetic rate law of Equation 1.1 is the failure of the rate to decrease
with increasing [Si] (which should result in decreasing reaction affinity) and, indeed, the
observation that the rate can even increase with increasing [Si]. For example, the dissolution of
IDF7- E12 under increasingly saturated conditions and 90°C showed a glass dissolution rate, as
indicated by the B release rate, that continued to increase with increasing orthosilicic
concentrations in the effluent solutions and reached values that exceeded the forward rate at the
specific pH and temperature. In stark contrast, Re dissolution sharply decreased and eventually
assumed a rate that was <10% of the corresponding glass dissolution rate. Deviations from the
kinetic rate law were also observed previously for LAW glass IDF1-B2.

 The above observations collectively highlight the importance of improved understanding
of the mechanisms of LAW glass dissolution and the limitations of the present kinetic rate law.
The rate law appears to be overly simplistic with respect to other more complex alteration

 43

mechanics that have been reported in the literature. From a pragmatic perspective, it is important
to understand whether the present rate law, despite its limitations, is sufficiently accurate for
supporting the IDF PA. However, since measurements on IDF7-E12 have showed that the
dissolution rate can increase with [Si], whereas the rate law would predict a decrease, it is
difficult to argue that use of the rate law is necessarily conservative.

 IDP-based glass dissolution has been reported for both highly corrosive (1M HCl, 100°C)
and relatively mild (deionized water, 50°C) conditions [15-17, 20, 28-30]. Furthermore, the lack
of inter-diffusion evidence and the presence of oscillating rims of SiO2, a hallmark of IDP, have
been observed with many long-term dissolution systems, altered under different environmental
conditions: dissolution of French glass SON68 at 90°C for >24 years, corrosion of Roman
glasses in seawater for ~2,000 years, and weathering of silicate minerals over geological time
scales. Consequently, the deviations of LAW glasses from the classical theories of glass
dissolution should be investigated for the role that may play in the alteration of these glasses at
the repository environment.

 A further question relates to relevance of the dissolution behavior at elevated
temperatures to the behavior at temperatures pertinent to the repository. The rate law in Equation
1.1 assumes that glass dissolution at elevated temperatures follows the same mechanism as at
lower temperatures. It is possible, however, that different mechanisms may dominate at different
temperatures. For example Stage III resumption of dissolution has been observed only at
elevated temperatures and there is an ongoing debate as to the relevance of the phenomenon to
typical repository temperatures. Consequently, in terms of determining the kinetic parameters for
LAW glasses, it may be worth considering substituting the 90°C testing for testing at an
intermediate temperature between 40 and 70°C. This substitution would focus the testing closer
to the range of interest and would be more likely to allow for the inclusion of four points in the
estimation of the Kg value at 15°C. In view of the very different behavior observed for boron and
rhenium, it is also recommended that Re2O7 be included in the compositions of all LAW glasses
selected for SPFT testing and that effluent solutions are subjected to Re analysis, at least for tests
performed under saturating conditions.

 Finally, it is clear that the McGrail convention for estimating ion exchange has
limitations and needs to be revised since studies have suggested that B dissolution appears to be
under the control of Na+/H+ exchange processes at many instances. Thus, simple subtraction of
the boron rate from the sodium rate is likely to underestimate the ion exchange rate.
